Input

{
  "filePath": "/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc"
}

Output

<path>/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Use when writing, reviewing, or refactoring code to avoid overcomplication, make surgical changes, surface assumptions, and define verifiable success criteria.
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Karpathy behavioral guidelines
7: 
8: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Merge with project-specific instructions as needed.
9: 
10: **Tradeoff:** These guidelines bias toward caution over speed. For trivial tasks, use judgment.
11: 
12: ## 1. Think Before Coding
13: 
14: **Don't assume. Don't hide confusion. Surface tradeoffs.**
15: 
16: Before implementing:
17: 
18: - State your assumptions explicitly. If uncertain, ask.
19: - If multiple interpretations exist, present them - don't pick silently.
20: - If a simpler approach exists, say so. Push back when warranted.
21: - If something is unclear, stop. Name what's confusing. Ask.
22: 
23: ## 2. Simplicity First
24: 
25: **Minimum code that solves the problem. Nothing speculative.**
26: 
27: - No features beyond what was asked.
28: - No abstractions for single-use code.
29: - No "flexibility" or "configurability" that wasn't requested.
30: - No error handling for impossible scenarios.
31: - If you write 200 lines and it could be 50, rewrite it.
32: 
33: Ask yourself: "Would a senior engineer say this is overcomplicated?" If yes, simplify.
34: 
35: ## 3. Surgical Changes
36: 
37: **Touch only what you must. Clean up only your own mess.**
38: 
39: When editing existing code:
40: 
41: - Don't "improve" adjacent code, comments, or formatting.
42: - Don't refactor things that aren't broken.
43: - Match existing style, even if you'd do it differently.
44: - If you notice unrelated dead code, mention it - don't delete it.
45: 
46: When your changes create orphans:
47: 
48: - Remove imports/variables/functions that YOUR changes made unused.
49: - Don't remove pre-existing dead code unless asked.
50: 
51: The test: Every changed line should trace directly to the user's request.
52: 
53: ## 4. Goal-Driven Execution
54: 
55: **Define success criteria. Loop until verified.**
56: 
57: Transform tasks into verifiable goals:
58: 
59: - "Add validation" → "Write tests for invalid inputs, then make them pass"
60: - "Fix the bug" → "Write a test that reproduces it, then make it pass"
61: - "Refactor X" → "Ensure tests pass before and after"
62: 
63: For multi-step tasks, state a brief plan:
64: 
65: ```
66: 1. [Step] → verify: [check]
67: 2. [Step] → verify: [check]
68: 3. [Step] → verify: [check]
69: ```
70: 
71: Strong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria ("make it work") require constant clarification.
72: 
73: ---
74: 
75: **These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.

(End of file - total 75 lines)
</content>

Input

{
  "filePath": "/source/standards/ponytail.mdc"
}

Output

<path>/source/standards/ponytail.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.
3: globs:
4: alwaysApply: true
5: ---
6: 
7: # Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode
8: 
9: You are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.
10: 
11: Before writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:
12: 
13: 1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)
14: 2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.
15: 3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.
16: 4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.
17: 5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.
18: 6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.
19: 7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.
20: 
21: The ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.
22: 
23: Bug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.
24: 
25: Rules:
26: 
27: - No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.
28: - No new dependency if it can be avoided.
29: - No boilerplate nobody asked for.
30: - Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.
31: - Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.
32: - Question complex requests: "Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?"
33: - Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.
34: - Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.
35: 
36: Not lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.

(End of file - total 36 lines)
</content>

Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.\n\n(End of file - total 36 lines)\n</content>",

        "text": "---\ndescription: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.\nglobs:\nalwaysApply: true\n---\n\n# Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode\n\nYou are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.\n\nBefore writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:\n\n1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)\n2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.\n3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.\n4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.\n5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.\n6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.\n7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.\n\nThe ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.\n\nBug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.\n\nRules:\n\n- No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.\n- No new dependency if it can be avoided.\n- No boilerplate nobody asked for.\n- Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.\n- Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.\n- Question complex requests: \"Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?\"\n- Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.\n- Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.\n\nNot lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.",

320: +// SanitizeLabelValue maps a containerlab name onto the value of a Kubernetes label: at most 63
321: +// characters, made up of a-z, A-Z, 0-9, '-', '_' and '.', starting and ending with an alphanumeric
322: +// character. Unlike SanitizeName it keeps the case the topology used, so a label selector matches
323: +// the name the clab file spells out verbatim.
324: +//
325: +// The mapping is deterministic and idempotent: a name Kubernetes already accepts as a label value
326: +// maps onto itself. The result is empty only for a name holding no character a label value can be
327: +// built from.
328: +func SanitizeLabelValue(name string) string {
329: +	if len(name) <= kubernetesNameMaxLen && kubernetesLabelValue.MatchString(name) {
330: +		return name
331: +	}
332: +
333: +	sanitized := strings.Trim(
334: +		nonKubernetesLabelValueChars.ReplaceAllString(name, "-"),
335: +		"-_.",
336: +	)
337: +	if sanitized == "" {
338: +		return ""
339: +	}
340: +
341: +	if len(sanitized) > kubernetesNameMaxLen {
342: +		digest := sha256.Sum256([]byte(name))
343: +		sanitized = strings.TrimRight(
344: +			sanitized[:kubernetesNameMaxLen-nameDigestLen-1],
345: +			"-_.",
346: +		) + "-" + hex.EncodeToString(digest[:])[:nameDigestLen]
347: +	}
348: +
349: +	return sanitized
350: +}

382: +			t.Parallel()

385: +			if got != tt.want {
386: +				t.Fatalf("SanitizeLabelValue(%q) = %q, want %q", tt.name, got, tt.want)
387: +			}
388: +			if again := SanitizeLabelValue(got); again != got {
389: +				t.Fatalf(

391: +				)

13: 	// kubernetesNameMaxLen is the maximum length of a DNS label, the form the object names a lab
14: 	// runtime derives from containerlab names have to take.
15: 	kubernetesNameMaxLen = 63
16: 	// nameDigestLen is the number of digest characters appended to a name that had to be
17: 	// truncated to fit kubernetesNameMaxLen.
18: 	nameDigestLen = 7
19: 	// leadingLetterPrefix prefixes a name that would otherwise start with a digit or a dash,
20: 	// which a DNS-1035 label cannot do.
21: 	leadingLetterPrefix = "clab-"
22: )
23: 
24: // nonKubernetesNameChars matches every run of characters a DNS label cannot carry, once the name
25: // has been lower-cased.
26: var nonKubernetesNameChars = regexp.MustCompile(`[^a-z0-9-]+`)
27: 
28: // nonKubernetesLabelValueChars matches every run of characters a Kubernetes label value cannot
29: // carry. Label values keep the case the name was written with.
30: var nonKubernetesLabelValueChars = regexp.MustCompile(`[^a-zA-Z0-9_.-]+`)
31: 
32: // kubernetesLabelValue matches the label values the Kubernetes API server accepts. It mirrors
33: // apimachinery's IsValidLabelValue so the names package stays free of Kubernetes dependencies.
34: var kubernetesLabelValue = regexp.MustCompile(`^[a-zA-Z0-9]([-a-zA-Z0-9_.]*[a-zA-Z0-9])?$`)
35: 
36: // SanitizeName maps a containerlab name onto the DNS-1035 label a lab runtime can name the objects
37: // it creates with: lower case, made up of a-z, 0-9 and '-', starting with a letter and at most 63
38: // characters long. A large share of public labs name their nodes R1/PE_1 or capitalize the lab
39: // name, and Kubernetes cannot carry those names as they are -- renaming them beats making the user
40: // edit every reference in the topology.
41: //
42: // The mapping is deterministic and idempotent: a name Kubernetes already accepts maps onto itself,
43: // so a sanitized name coming back from the runtime -- or typed by a user reading it off
44: // containerlab output -- resolves to the same object. The result is empty only for a name holding
45: // nothing a Kubernetes name can be built from.
46: func SanitizeName(name string) string {
47: 	sanitized := strings.Trim(
48: 		nonKubernetesNameChars.ReplaceAllString(strings.ToLower(name), "-"),
49: 		"-",
50: 	)
51: 	if sanitized == "" {
52: 		return ""
53: 	}
54: 
55: 	if sanitized[0] < 'a' || sanitized[0] > 'z' {
56: 		sanitized = leadingLetterPrefix + sanitized
57: 	}
58: 
59: 	if len(sanitized) > kubernetesNameMaxLen {
60: 		digest := sha256.Sum256([]byte(name))
61: 		sanitized = strings.TrimRight(
62: 			sanitized[:kubernetesNameMaxLen-nameDigestLen-1],
63: 			"-",
64: 		) + "-" + hex.EncodeToString(digest[:])[:nameDigestLen]
65: 	}
66: 
67: 	return sanitized
68: }
69: 
70: // SanitizeNodeNames returns the sanitized name of every node name that needs one, keyed by the name
71: // the topology uses. Node names Kubernetes can carry as they are are absent from the result.
72: //
73: // Two node names that differ only in something Kubernetes cannot carry (R1 and r1) would collapse
74: // onto one object, so that is reported as an error instead of silently merging the two nodes.
75: func SanitizeNodeNames(nodeNames []string) (map[string]string, error) {
76: 	renames := map[string]string{}
77: 	origins := make(map[string]string, len(nodeNames))
78: 
79: 	sorted := slices.Clone(nodeNames)
80: 	slices.Sort(sorted)
81: 
82: 	for _, nodeName := range sorted {
83: 		sanitized := SanitizeName(nodeName)
84: 		if sanitized == "" {
85: 			return nil, fmt.Errorf(
86: 				"node name %q holds no character a Kubernetes object name can be built from",
87: 				nodeName,
88: 			)
89: 		}
90: 
91: 		if origin, taken := origins[sanitized]; taken {
92: 			return nil, fmt.Errorf(
93: 				"node names %q and %q both map onto the Kubernetes name %q; rename one of them",
94: 				origin,
95: 				nodeName,
96: 				sanitized,
97: 			)
98: 		}
99: 		origins[sanitized] = nodeName
100: 
101: 		if sanitized != nodeName {
102: 			renames[nodeName] = sanitized
103: 		}
104: 	}
105: 
106: 	return renames, nil
107: }
108: 
109: // SanitizeLabelValue maps a containerlab name onto the value of a Kubernetes label: at most 63
110: // characters, made up of a-z, A-Z, 0-9, '-', '_' and '.', starting and ending with an alphanumeric
111: // character. Unlike SanitizeName it keeps the case the topology used, so a label selector matches
112: // the name the clab file spells out verbatim.
113: //
114: // The mapping is deterministic and idempotent: a name Kubernetes already accepts as a label value
115: // maps onto itself. The result is empty only for a name holding no character a label value can be
116: // built from.
117: func SanitizeLabelValue(name string) string {
118: 	if len(name) <= kubernetesNameMaxLen && kubernetesLabelValue.MatchString(name) {
119: 		return name
120: 	}
121: 
122: 	sanitized := strings.Trim(
123: 		nonKubernetesLabelValueChars.ReplaceAllString(name, "-"),
124: 		"-_.",
125: 	)
126: 	if sanitized == "" {
127: 		return ""
128: 	}
129: 
130: 	if len(sanitized) > kubernetesNameMaxLen {
131: 		digest := sha256.Sum256([]byte(name))
132: 		sanitized = strings.TrimRight(
133: 			sanitized[:kubernetesNameMaxLen-nameDigestLen-1],
134: 			"-_.",
135: 		) + "-" + hex.EncodeToString(digest[:])[:nameDigestLen]
136: 	}
137: 
138: 	return sanitized
139: }

(End of file - total 139 lines)

1: package clabernetes
2: 
3: import (
4: 	"strings"
5: 
6: 	clabernetescompiler "github.com/clabernetes/clabernetes/compiler"
7: 	clablinks "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/links"
8: 	clabnames "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/names"
9: 	clabtypes "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/types"
10: )
11: 
12: // sanitizeNodeNames renames the topology nodes Kubernetes cannot carry, along with every reference
13: // to them the c9s subset understands, and reports what was renamed. c9s uses containerlab node
14: // names verbatim as Node, Deployment and Service names, so a node named R1 has to become r1 before
15: // anything is created; the alternative is rejecting a large share of public labs outright.
16: //
17: // Every node definition also carries c9s.run/originalNodeName naming the node as the topology file
18: // spells it, so tooling holding only the clab file can select the c9s Nodes by the names they use
19: // there. The label rides in the definition, which is what makes it survive both the Topology
20: // resource path (the controller compiles it in-cluster) and the primitives path (compiled here);
21: // the compiler stamps the same label on Nodes it renames itself, for topologies authored outside
22: // containerlab.
23: //
24: // The boolean reports whether the topology definition was modified, so the caller only re-renders
25: // the definition when it really changed.
26: func sanitizeNodeNames(
27: 	config *clabRuntimeConfig,
28: 	nodeNames []string,
29: ) (map[string]string, bool, error) {
30: 	renames, err := clabnames.SanitizeNodeNames(nodeNames)
31: 	if err != nil {
32: 		return nil, false, err
33: 	}
34: 
35: 	changed := len(renames) != 0
36: 
37: 	nodes := make(map[string]*clabtypes.NodeDefinition, len(config.Topology.Nodes))
38: 	for originalName, nodeDefinition := range config.Topology.Nodes {
39: 		nodeName := originalName
40: 		if sanitized, renamed := renames[originalName]; renamed {
41: 			nodeName = sanitized
42: 		}
43: 
44: 		originalLabel := clabnames.SanitizeLabelValue(originalName)
45: 		if nodeDefinition.Labels[labelOriginalNodeName] != originalLabel {
46: 			if nodeDefinition.Labels == nil {
47: 				nodeDefinition.Labels = map[string]string{}
48: 			}
49: 			nodeDefinition.Labels[labelOriginalNodeName] = originalLabel
50: 			changed = true
51: 		}
52: 
53: 		nodes[nodeName] = nodeDefinition
54: 	}
55: 	config.Topology.Nodes = nodes
56: 
57: 	if len(renames) != 0 {
58: 		// A node can share the network namespace of another node by name, and the compiler resolves
59: 		// that setting through the kind, group and defaults sections as well.
60: 		for _, nodeDefinition := range referencingNodeDefinitions(config.Topology) {
61: 			clabernetescompiler.RenameNetworkModePrimary(nodeDefinition, renames)
62: 		}
63: 	}
64: 
65: 	return renames, changed, nil
66: }
67: 
68: // referencingNodeDefinitions returns every node definition of a topology that can hold a reference
69: // to a node name, in no particular order.
70: func referencingNodeDefinitions(topology *clabtypes.Topology) []*clabtypes.NodeDefinition {
71: 	definitions := make(
72: 		[]*clabtypes.NodeDefinition,
73: 		0,
74: 		len(topology.Nodes)+len(topology.Kinds)+len(topology.Groups)+1,
75: 	)
76: 	definitions = append(definitions, topology.Defaults)
77: 
78: 	for _, group := range []map[string]*clabtypes.NodeDefinition{
79: 		topology.Nodes,
80: 		topology.Kinds,
81: 		topology.Groups,
82: 	} {
83: 		for _, nodeDefinition := range group {
84: 			definitions = append(definitions, nodeDefinition)
85: 		}
86: 	}
87: 
88: 	return definitions
89: }
90: 
91: // renameBriefLinkEndpoints points the wiring at the sanitized node names. Endpoints that do not
92: // name a renamed node are left alone, which is what keeps the host, mgmt-net and macvlan pseudo
93: // endpoints intact.
94: func renameBriefLinkEndpoints(links []*clablinks.LinkBriefRaw, renames map[string]string) {
95: 	if len(renames) == 0 {
96: 		return
97: 	}
98: 
99: 	for _, link := range links {
100: 		endpoints := make([]string, 0, len(link.Endpoints))
101: 
102: 		for _, endpoint := range link.Endpoints {
103: 			nodeName, interfaceName, hasInterface := strings.Cut(endpoint, ":")
104: 
105: 			sanitized, renamed := renames[nodeName]
106: 			switch {
107: 			case !renamed:
108: 				endpoints = append(endpoints, endpoint)
109: 			case hasInterface:
110: 				endpoints = append(endpoints, sanitized+":"+interfaceName)
111: 			default:
112: 				endpoints = append(endpoints, sanitized)
113: 			}
114: 		}
115: 
116: 		link.Endpoints = endpoints
117: 	}
118: }
119: 
120: // renameStagedConfigMapNodes points the staged file projections at the sanitized node names. The
121: // ConfigMaps themselves are shared by source file, but each mount and ownership reference has to
122: // match the Node object c9s creates.
123: func renameStagedConfigMapNodes(configMaps []stagedConfigMap, renames map[string]string) {
124: 	if len(renames) == 0 {
125: 		return
126: 	}
127: 
128: 	for idx := range configMaps {
129: 		for mountIdx := range configMaps[idx].mounts {
130: 			mount := &configMaps[idx].mounts[mountIdx]

133: 			}
134: 		}

138: // resolveKnownNodeName maps a node name a user supplied onto the name the lab carries in
139: // Kubernetes. Node names Kubernetes cannot carry are sanitized at deploy time, so a name typed
140: // exactly as the topology file writes it has to keep resolving. A name that exists verbatim always
141: // wins: a primitive-only lab created outside containerlab may legitimately carry a name the
142: // sanitizer would map elsewhere.
143: func resolveKnownNodeName[V any](known map[string]V, nodeName string) (string, bool) {
144: 	if _, ok := known[nodeName]; ok {
145: 		return nodeName, true
146: 	}
147: 
148: 	sanitized := clabnames.SanitizeName(nodeName)
149: 	if _, ok := known[sanitized]; ok {
150: 		return sanitized, true
151: 	}
152: 
153: 	return "", false
154: }

K8s label values: must be 63 characters or less, begin and end with alphanumeric character ([a-z0-9A-Z]), with dashes (-), underscores (_), dots (.), and alphanumerics between. The regex `^[a-zA-Z0-9]([-a-zA-Z0-9_.]*[a-zA-Z0-9])?$` — for a single char, matches. OK. But the regex mirrors IsValidLabelValue which requires length ≤ 63; the function checks `len(name) <= kubernetesNameMaxLen` before MatchString. Good.

Potential issue: SanitizeLabelValue replaces disallowed chars with "-" then trims "-_." from both ends. But trimming only removes at ends — but after replacement, interior characters are all valid, so only ends can be invalid. Fine. However, what about a name that consists of valid chars but starts with "-"? e.g., "-leaf-" → after replacement unchanged (all chars are valid) but doesn't match the valid regex (starts with -), so Trim removes leading/trailing "-" → "leaf". Good.

But wait: what if the name after trimming still doesn't match, e.g., name = "+"? Replaced with "-" → trimmed → "" → returns "". OK.

So consistent. If name = "-.": both empty → error. OK so in this flow, empty labels can't happen. Fine.

But wait — a node whose name is e.g. "a" vs... anyway.

Now the more interesting issue: does `kubernetesLabelValue.MatchString` have the right regex? Real K8s: `(([A-Za-z0-9][-A-Za-z0-9_.]*)?[A-Za-z0-9])?`. The mirror is fine.

2. Now check `labelOriginalNodeName` added to node definition labels: this mutates the user's parsed topology config in-place and then the definition is re-rendered and stored in the Topology resource. The test expects the stored definition to include the label. Any concern: this writes into `config.Topology.Nodes[...].Labels` map which may be shared with the actual containerlab runtime config used elsewhere (e.g., the same NodeDefinition objects are used by the local containerlab deploy to create the real containers). Adding a label `c9s.run/originalNodeName` to node definitions — could it leak into container labels or other resources in non-clabernetes paths? This code is in labruntime/clabernetes, only used in the clabernetes runtime path. Within the c9s path, the config is used to build the Topology resource / primitives. The label rides in the definition, so the compiler will put it on Node objects. That's intended.

But wait: does mutating nodeDefinition.Labels leak into anything user-visible like `containerlab inspect` output or node data? In c9s runtime the local config isn't used for local containers. Probably fine.

3. Potential bug: in sanitizeNodeNames, the label check `nodeDefinition.Labels[labelOriginalNodeName] != originalLabel` — if the user's topology explicitly sets a label `c9s.run/originalNodeName` with a different value, it gets overwritten silently. Edge case; user labels live under `labels` in node config? In containerlab, node definitions have `labels` map. A user who deliberately sets `c9s.run/originalNodeName` would have it clobbered. Minor. Also if the user sets it to the correct value, `changed` remains false — fine, idempotent.

4. Bigger potential issue: `changed` is set to true when the label is added, but is `definitionsChanged` correct when only the label was added but renames are empty? Then `renames` is empty, and renderClabernetesTopologyDefinition is called with empty renames → renameBriefLinkEndpoints no-op → renders the definition including the label. But note: `definitionChanged` was already `len(config.Topology.Links) > 0` at the start. So with links present, re-render always happens anyway.

But wait — there's an ordering issue: in the previous code, `sanitizeNodeNames` mutated config.Topology.Nodes (rebuilding map with renamed keys). Now it also mutates nodeDefinition.Labels. If `definitionChanged` is false (no links, no renames, no label change), topologyDefinition = req.TopologyDefinition (the original raw YAML), which would NOT include the label — but that only happens when no label was added, so consistent.

Hmm wait, there is a subtle case: renames non-empty triggers changed. Label addition triggers changed. But what about the case where labels were added but... fine.

5. Another subtle issue: the mutation of `nodeDefinition.Labels` happens even when it's a `topology.Defaults`, `Kinds`, `Groups` node? No — only `config.Topology.Nodes` entries get labels. referencingNodeDefinitions includes Defaults/Kinds/Groups but the label loop only iterates config.Topology.Nodes. But careful: in containerlab types, `topology.Defaults` may be the same pointer as one of the Nodes? Possibly (some YAML unmarshalling shares). Not important.

6. Now — the important correctness check: does the *stored Topology definition* need the label to survive? The test expects stored definition to contain labels. And the controller (external clabernetes lib v0.10.1) now stamps the label too. The go.mod bump to v0.10.1 presumably provides SanitizeLabelValue-stamping in the compiler. We can't inspect the external lib.

7. Check `renames` unused variable in files.go? It's used for renameStagedConfigMapNodes and renderClabernetesTopologyDefinition.

8. Now check the head clabernetes.go for the const addition and any other uses of labelOriginalNodeName.

<path>/source/base/labruntime/clabernetes/names.go</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: package clabernetes
2: 
3: import (
4: 	"strings"
5: 
6: 	clabernetescompiler "github.com/clabernetes/clabernetes/compiler"
7: 	clablinks "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/links"
8: 	clabnames "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/names"
9: 	clabtypes "github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/types"
10: )
11: 
12: // sanitizeNodeNames renames the topology nodes Kubernetes cannot carry, along with every reference
13: // to them the c9s subset understands, and reports what was renamed. c9s uses containerlab node
14: // names verbatim as Node, Deployment and Service names, so a node named R1 has to become r1 before
15: // anything is created; the alternative is rejecting a large share of public labs outright.
16: func sanitizeNodeNames(
17: 	config *clabRuntimeConfig,
18: 	nodeNames []string,
19: ) (map[string]string, error) {
20: 	renames, err := clabnames.SanitizeNodeNames(nodeNames)
21: 	if err != nil || len(renames) == 0 {
22: 		return nil, err
23: 	}
24: 
25: 	nodes := make(map[string]*clabtypes.NodeDefinition, len(config.Topology.Nodes))
26: 	for nodeName, nodeDefinition := range config.Topology.Nodes {
27: 		if sanitized, renamed := renames[nodeName]; renamed {
28: 			nodeName = sanitized
29: 		}
30: 
31: 		nodes[nodeName] = nodeDefinition
32: 	}
33: 	config.Topology.Nodes = nodes
34: 
35: 	// A node can share the network namespace of another node by name, and the compiler resolves
36: 	// that setting through the kind, group and defaults sections as well.
37: 	for _, nodeDefinition := range referencingNodeDefinitions(config.Topology) {
38: 		clabernetescompiler.RenameNetworkModePrimary(nodeDefinition, renames)
39: 	}
40: 
41: 	return renames, nil
42: }
43: 
44: // referencingNodeDefinitions returns every node definition of a topology that can hold a reference
45: // to a node name, in no particular order.
46: func referencingNodeDefinitions(topology *clabtypes.Topology) []*clabtypes.NodeDefinition {
47: 	definitions := make(
48: 		[]*clabtypes.NodeDefinition,
49: 		0,
50: 		len(topology.Nodes)+len(topology.Kinds)+len(topology.Groups)+1,
51: 	)
52: 	definitions = append(definitions, topology.Defaults)
53: 
54: 	for _, group := range []map[string]*clabtypes.NodeDefinition{
55: 		topology.Nodes,
56: 		topology.Kinds,
57: 		topology.Groups,
58: 	} {
59: 		for _, nodeDefinition := range group {
60: 			definitions = append(definitions, nodeDefinition)
61: 		}
62: 	}
63: 
64: 	return definitions
65: }
66: 
67: // renameBriefLinkEndpoints points the wiring at the sanitized node names. Endpoints that do not
68: // name a renamed node are left alone, which is what keeps the host, mgmt-net and macvlan pseudo
69: // endpoints intact.
70: func renameBriefLinkEndpoints(links []*clablinks.LinkBriefRaw, renames map[string]string) {
71: 	if len(renames) == 0 {
72: 		return
73: 	}
74: 
75: 	for _, link := range links {
76: 		endpoints := make([]string, 0, len(link.Endpoints))
77: 
78: 		for _, endpoint := range link.Endpoints {
79: 			nodeName, interfaceName, hasInterface := strings.Cut(endpoint, ":")
80: 
81: 			sanitized, renamed := renames[nodeName]
82: 			switch {
83: 			case !renamed:
84: 				endpoints = append(endpoints, endpoint)
85: 			case hasInterface:
86: 				endpoints = append(endpoints, sanitized+":"+interfaceName)
87: 			default:
88: 				endpoints = append(endpoints, sanitized)
89: 			}
90: 		}
91: 
92: 		link.Endpoints = endpoints
93: 	}
94: }
95: 
96: // renameStagedConfigMapNodes points the staged file projections at the sanitized node names. The
97: // ConfigMaps themselves are shared by source file, but each mount and ownership reference has to
98: // match the Node object c9s creates.
99: func renameStagedConfigMapNodes(configMaps []stagedConfigMap, renames map[string]string) {
100: 	if len(renames) == 0 {
101: 		return
102: 	}
103: 
104: 	for idx := range configMaps {
105: 		for mountIdx := range configMaps[idx].mounts {
106: 			mount := &configMaps[idx].mounts[mountIdx]
107: 			if sanitized, renamed := renames[mount.nodeName]; renamed {
108: 				mount.nodeName = sanitized
109: 			}
110: 		}
111: 	}
112: }
113: 
114: // resolveKnownNodeName maps a node name a user supplied onto the name the lab carries in
115: // Kubernetes. Node names Kubernetes cannot carry are sanitized at deploy time, so a name typed
116: // exactly as the topology file writes it has to keep resolving. A name that exists verbatim always
117: // wins: a primitive-only lab created outside containerlab may legitimately carry a name the
118: // sanitizer would map elsewhere.
119: func resolveKnownNodeName[V any](known map[string]V, nodeName string) (string, bool) {
120: 	if _, ok := known[nodeName]; ok {
121: 		return nodeName, true
122: 	}
123: 
124: 	sanitized := clabnames.SanitizeName(nodeName)
125: 	if _, ok := known[sanitized]; ok {
126: 		return sanitized, true
127: 	}
128:

<path>/source/base/names/names.go</path>

<content>
1: package names
2: 
3: import (
4: 	"crypto/sha256"
5: 	"encoding/hex"
6: 	"fmt"
7: 	"regexp"
8: 	"slices"
9: 	"strings"
10: )
11: 
12: const (
13: 	// kubernetesNameMaxLen is the maximum length of a DNS label, the form the object names a lab
14: 	// runtime derives from containerlab names have to take.
15: 	kubernetesNameMaxLen = 63
16: 	// nameDigestLen is the number of digest characters appended to a name that had to be
17: 	// truncated to fit kubernetesNameMaxLen.
18: 	nameDigestLen = 7
19: 	// leadingLetterPrefix prefixes a name that would otherwise start with a digit or a dash,
20: 	// which a DNS-1035 label cannot do.
21: 	leadingLetterPrefix = "clab-"
22: )
23: 
24: // nonKubernetesNameChars matches every run of characters a DNS label cannot carry, once the name
25: // has been lower-cased.
26: var nonKubernetesNameChars = regexp.MustCompile(`[^a-z0-9-]+`)
27: 
28: // SanitizeName maps a containerlab name onto the DNS-1035 label a lab runtime can name the objects
29: // it creates with: lower case, made up of a-z, 0-9 and '-', starting with a letter and at most 63
30: // characters long. A large share of public labs name their nodes R1/PE_1 or capitalize the lab
31: // name, and Kubernetes cannot carry those names as they are -- renaming them beats making the user
32: // edit every reference in the topology.
33: //
34: // The mapping is deterministic and idempotent: a name Kubernetes already accepts maps onto itself,
35: // so a sanitized name coming back from the runtime -- or typed by a user reading it off
36: // containerlab output -- resolves to the same object. The result is empty only for a name holding
37: // nothing a Kubernetes name can be built from.
38: func SanitizeName(name string) string {
39: 	sanitized := strings.Trim(
40: 		nonKubernetesNameChars.ReplaceAllString(strings.ToLower(name), "-"),
41: 		"-",
42: 	)
43: 	if sanitized == "" {
44: 		return ""
45: 	}
46: 
47: 	if sanitized[0] < 'a' || sanitized[0] > 'z' {
48: 		sanitized = leadingLetterPrefix + sanitized
49: 	}
50: 
51: 	if len(sanitized) > kubernetesNameMaxLen {
52: 		digest := sha256.Sum256([]byte(name))
53: 		sanitized = strings.TrimRight(
54: 			sanitized[:kubernetesNameMaxLen-nameDigestLen-1],
55: 			"-",
56: 		) + "-" + hex.EncodeToString(digest[:])[:nameDigestLen]
57: 	}
58: 
59: 	return sanitized
60: }
61: 
62: // SanitizeNodeNames returns the sanitized name of every node name that needs one, keyed by the name
63: // the topology uses. Node names Kubernetes can carry as they are are absent from the result.
64: //
65: // Two node names that differ only in something Kubernetes cannot carry (R1 and r1) would collapse
66: // onto one object, so that is reported as an error instead of silently merging the two nodes.
67: func SanitizeNodeNames(nodeNames []string) (map[string]string, error) {
68: 	renames := map[string]string{}
69: 	origins := make(map[string]string, len(nodeNames))
70: 
71: 	sorted := slices.Clone(nodeNames)
72: 	slices.Sort(sorted)
73: 
74: 	for _, nodeName := range sorted {
75: 		sanitized := SanitizeName(nodeName)
76: 		if sanitized == "" {
77: 			return nil, fmt.Errorf(
78: 				"node name %q holds no character a Kubernetes object name can be built from",
79: 				nodeName,
80: 			)
81: 		}
82: 
83: 		if origin, taken := origins[sanitized]; taken {
84: 			return nil, fmt.Errorf(
85: 				"node names %q and %q both map onto the Kubernetes name %q; rename one of them",
86: 				origin,
87: 				nodeName,
88: 				sanitized,
89: 			)
90: 		}
91: 		origins[sanitized] = nodeName
92: 
93: 		if sanitized != nodeName {
94: 			renames[nodeName] = sanitized
95: 		}
96: 	}
97: 
98: 	return renames, nil
99: }

(End of file - total 99 lines)

Node names Kubernetes can carry as they are are absent from the result.\n//\n// Two node names that differ only in something Kubernetes cannot carry (R1 and r1) would collapse\n// onto one object, so that is reported as an error instead of silently merging the two nodes.\nfunc SanitizeNodeNames(nodeNames []string) (map[string]string, error) {\n\trenames := map[string]string{}\n\torigins := make(map[string]string, len(nodeNames))\n\n\tsorted := slices.Clone(nodeNames)\n\tslices.Sort(sorted)\n\n\tfor _, nodeName := range sorted {\n\t\tsanitized := SanitizeName(nodeName)\n\t\tif sanitized == \"\" {\n\t\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\n\t\t\t\t\"node name %q holds no character a Kubernetes object name can be built from\",\n\t\t\t\tnodeName,\n\t\t\t)\n\t\t}\n\n\t\tif origin, taken := origins[sanitized]; taken {\n\t\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\n\t\t\t\t\"node names %q and %q both map onto the Kubernetes name %q; rename one of them\",\n\t\t\t\torigin,\n\t\t\t\tnodeName,\n\t\t\t\tsanitized,\n\t\t\t)\n\t\t}\n\t\torigins[sanitized] = nodeName\n\n\t\tif sanitized != nodeName {\n\t\t\trenames[nodeName] = sanitized\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\treturn renames, nil\n}",
        "lineStart": 1,
        "lineEnd": 99,
        "totalLines": 99,
        "truncated": false
      }
    },

Reasoning
Check `clabernetesRenderTopology` struct — what types are Nodes/Defaults/Kinds/Groups? Likely map[string]*NodeDefinition. And how the yaml marshal includes Labels (yaml tag `labels,omitempty`). Also check `stageStartupConfig` uses `config.Topology.GetNodeStartupConfig(nodeName)` with the *original* node name — the note says staging happens before sanitization, so lookups use original names. Good.

Important: is there a roundtrip issue — the sanitized name lookup: after sanitizeNodeNames renames keys to sanitized names, later code (e.g., primitives compile, staged configmaps rename) uses sanitized names. Fine.

Now the key question of whether storing the label in the definition causes churn: each deploy re-renders the definition with labels. `definitionChanged` initial is `len(config.Topology.Links) > 0`. If a topology has no links and no renames and labels already match, definition unchanged → original definition stored. Fine.

Now consider `TestDeployReconcilesExistingTopology`: existing definition has node1 without label; new deploy adds label → definitionChanged → re-render → updated. OK.

Potential issue #1: `SanitizeLabelValue` collision scenario between two distinct node names that sanitize to the same label value but whose SanitizeName results differ (so no SanitizeNodeNames error). Let me think harder. Label sanitization: keep [A-Za-z0-9_.-], replace other runs with "-", trim "-_.". Name sanitization: lowercase, keep [a-z0-9-], replace others (incl. uppercase, _, .) with "-", trim "-".

For two names to have equal SanitizeLabelValue but different SanitizeName: SanitizeName applies more transformations, so equal labels... Suppose name1 = "A-B", name2 = "A-B " — label: "A-B " → replace " "→"-" → "A-B-" → trim → "A-B". Name: "A-B " → "a-b-" → trim → "a-b". Equal names too. Hmm.

Suppose name1 = "a.b", name2 = "a-b". Label: "a.b" (valid) vs "a-b" — different labels. Good.

Where could labels collide but names not? Label keeps case; name folds case. So if two names differ only in case, labels differ, names collide → error. Names differing in _ vs . vs - : labels differ ("_" vs "." vs "-" all kept distinctly!). Wait — labels keep underscores and dots! So name2 "a_b" vs name3 "a-b": labels "a_b" vs "a-b" — different. But SanitizeName maps both to "a-b" → collision error. So SanitizeNodeNames is stricter.

Labels colliding requires: after replacement of invalid runs with "-" and trimming, same string. Names colliding requires lowercase version with _ and . folded to - equal. If labels equal, then... the labels equal as strings. Consider name1 = "a" and name2 = "a" — same. Consider truncation edge: name1 = 63-char valid label "X", name2 = long name whose label truncation = X? As computed, that requires digest equality (sha256 of different names differ). Actually not necessarily: truncation result = prefix55 + "-" + digest7. For the result to equal X (63 chars, valid), X must be exactly prefix55+"-"+digest7. If X is a node name in the topology, SanitizeLabelValue(X) = X (valid, ≤63). So SanitizeLabelValue(name2) = X = SanitizeLabelValue(X). Collision in labels. And names: SanitizeName(name2) = lowercase-sanitized truncated = lowercase(prefix)... name2's sanitized = nonK8sReplace(name2 lower) → prefix, truncated to 55, trimRight "-", + "-" + digest(name2). SanitizeName(X) = X if X is already a valid DNS name... X = prefix55+"-"+digest7; is it a valid DNS-1035 name? starts with letter (if prefix does), chars: depends on prefix55 — could contain "_" or "."? prefix comes from name2's sanitized which retains "_" and "." (label chars). E.g. name2 = "a.b"*30 (90 chars). Sanitized label: "a.b"*... → "a.bab.ba..." replaced? "." is valid so kept: sanitized = "a.b" repeated, trimmed → length 90 → truncate to 55: "a.bab.b..." (55 chars, alternating). digest7 of name2. X = that 63-char string. SanitizeName(X): lowercase, "." → "-" → "a-bab-ba-b..."-digest. SanitizeName(name2) = same lowercase-folded prefix truncated to 55 + "-" + digest(name2) — same! So names collide too → error. Hmm interesting: because name2's SanitizeName prefix is the lowercase-folded version of the label prefix (folding _ and . to -, lowercasing), and X lowercased-folded gives exactly the same (since X = prefix + "-" + digest, folding prefix = folded prefix). So they collide in names too → deploy error. So no new collision class introduced beyond what SanitizeNodeNames already errors on... Actually wait, one more: names differing only by characters that are valid in labels but map to the same in names AND produce non-colliding labels? Labels keep _ . - distinctly, so "a_b" vs "a-b" produce different labels. And names collide → error at SanitizeNodeNames. Fine.

What about label trimming of "_" and "." at ends: name1 = "a_" vs name2 = "a". Label: "a_" → trim → "a" = "a". Names: "a_" → "a-" → trim → "a" = "a". Both collide → error. Fine.

So SanitizeLabelValue collisions are subsumed by SanitizeNodeNames collisions for actual node names in the topology. But SanitizeLabelValue is a general API; only caller is sanitizeNodeNames with topology node names. OK — no collision issue in practice.

Potential issue #2: user-provided labels collision — if a user already sets `c9s.run/originalNodeName` for their own purposes, it's silently overwritten. Minor (P4-ish, arguably intended namespace c9s.run is reserved). I'll consider whether to report. The namespace is c9s.run which is the clabernetes-owned domain; the compiler "stamps the same label on Nodes it renames itself" per the doc comment. So overwriting user values under a reserved namespace is defensible. Probably not worth a finding, or P4.

Potential issue #3: In `sanitizeNodeNames`, the label comparison happens against `nodeDefinition.Labels[labelOriginalNodeName]` — but `nodeDefinition` may be nil? `config.Topology.Nodes` values — could a node definition be nil? In base code, `nodes[nodeName] = nodeDefinition` without nil check. If YAML has a node with no fields, yaml.Unmarshal into map[string]*NodeDefinition gives non-nil pointer probably (unmarshal allocates). If `nodes:` empty mapping? Then no entries. Likely fine.

Wait — actually there's a subtle nil-deref: `nodeDefinition.Labels[labelOriginalNodeName]` — if `nodeDefinition` is nil, this panics. In base code, `nodeDefinition` was only used as map value (no field access)... actually base used it only for pointer passing. So head introduces a field access on possibly-nil pointer. Can a YAML `topology: nodes: {node1:}` produce nil *NodeDefinition? yaml.v3 unmarshalling `{node1: }` (null value) into map[string]*NodeDefinition sets the value to nil! Yes — with gopkg.in/yaml.v3, a null value for a pointer field/map value leaves it nil. So a topology like:

```
topology:
  nodes:
    node1:
```

would produce `config.Topology.Nodes["node1"] = nil`? Actually for null mapping value with pointer type, yaml.v3 sets nil. Then `nodeDefinition.Labels` panics. But wait — does containerlab elsewhere guard against null node definitions? Real containerlab parses the topology into NodeDefinition structs by name, not pointers, typically. Here clabRuntimeConfig.Topology.Nodes is map[string]*clabtypes.NodeDefinition. Hmm, let me check types: Topology.Nodes type.

Reasoning

Hmm, actually GetNodeStartupConfig looks up the node; if the node's definition is nil, does it panic? Let me check `GetNodeStartupConfig`. But regardless, the guards at 337/364/404 suggest the authors defensively handle nil definitions in this package. The new code in sanitizeNodeNames does `nodeDefinition.Labels[...]` without a nil guard.

Can `config.Topology.Nodes` contain a nil value? With yaml.v3, `nodes:\n  node1:\n` (null value) unmarshals to map[string]*NodeDefinition with nil value. Let me verify whether earlier stages would panic first. stageTopologyLocalFiles: `if config.Topology == nil || len(config.Topology.Nodes) == 0` → proceeds. nodeNames collects keys including "node1". Then stageStartupConfig calls config.Topology.GetNodeStartupConfig("node1"). Let me check that method for nil handling.

Reasoning
The types package explicitly guards nil `nodeDefinition` in `getField`/`getFieldPtr`/`mergeStringMapFields` ("if nodeDefinition != nil"). This strongly implies that `topo.Nodes` can contain nil `*NodeDefinition` values (e.g., YAML `node1:` with a null body). And `files.go` guards at lines 337/364/404 for the same reason.

The new code in `sanitizeNodeNames` at labruntime/clabernetes/names.go:45 does `nodeDefinition.Labels[labelOriginalNodeName]` without a nil check → nil pointer dereference panic for a topology like:

```yaml
topology:
  nodes:
    node1:
```

Wait — would such a topology even reach sanitizeNodeNames? Deploy path: stageTopologyLocalFiles parses the definition; node1 present with nil definition. stageStartupConfig: GetNodeStartupConfig returns "" (getField checks ok — the key exists so it proceeds to `if nodeDefinition != nil` guard, then group/kind lookups; GetNodeGroup(nil node) → GetNodeGroup calls getField with GetNodeGroup(nodeName) as group name... GetNodeKind(nodeName) → getField returns kind from... for a nil node definition, GetNodeKind returns kind="" unless group/kind. Anyway returns "" → stageStartupConfig returns early. Same for license. stageBindFiles/stageAdditionalNodeFiles — check for nil guards? They probably call config.Topology.Nodes[nodeName] and read fields... Let me check stageBindFiles quickly for nil deref. Actually base code already ran through these stages with possibly-nil definitions before this PR, and if it panics there, it'd panic before reaching sanitizeNodeNames anyway. Let me check stageBindFiles and stageAdditionalNodeFiles.

451: 	mountPath := filepath.Join("/clabernetes", nodeName, field,
452: 		filepath.Base(clabutils.FilenameForURL(ctx, sourcePath)))
453: 	staged, err := loadStagedLocalFile(mountPath, file.Name(), info)
454: 	if err != nil {
455: 		return "", nil, err
456: 	}
457: 
458: 	return mountPath, []stagedLocalFile{staged}, nil

461: func stageBindFiles(
462: 	config *clabRuntimeConfig,
463: 	topologyName,
464: 	nodeName,
465: 	topologyFileDir,
466: 	topologyLabDir string,
467: 	configMaps map[string]*stagedConfigMap,
468: ) error {
469: 	binds, err := config.Topology.GetNodeBinds(nodeName)
470: 	if err != nil {
471: 		return fmt.Errorf("failed parsing bind mounts for node %q: %w", nodeName, err)
472: 	}
473: 	if len(binds) == 0 {
474: 		return nil
475: 	}
476: 
477: 	for _, bind := range binds {
478: 		parsedBind, err := clabtypes.NewBindFromString(bind)
479: 		if err != nil {
480: 			return fmt.Errorf("failed parsing bind %q for node %q: %w", bind, nodeName, err)
481: 		}
482: 		if parsedBind.Src() == "" {
483: 			continue
484: 		}
485: 
486: 		if err := stageSourcePathIntoConfigMaps(
487: 			configMaps,
488: 			topologyName,
489: 			parsedBind.Src(),
490: 			nodeName,
491: 			topologyFileDir,
492: 			topologyLabDir,
493: 		); err != nil {
494: 			return err
495: 		}
496: 	}
497: 
498: 	return nil
499: }

501: // stageAdditionalNodeFiles covers path-bearing fields that containerlab normally resolves on
502: // the machine running the CLI. A device pod cannot see those paths unless the adapter copies
503: // them into the node's ConfigMap-backed file projection first.
504: func stageAdditionalNodeFiles(
505: 	config *clabRuntimeConfig,
506: 	topologyName,
507: 	nodeName,
508: 	topologyFileDir,
509: 	topologyLabDir string,
510: 	configMaps map[string]*stagedConfigMap,
511: ) error {
512: 	pathsByField := map[string][]string{
513: 		"env-files": config.Topology.GetNodeEnvFiles(nodeName),
514: 	}
515: 
516: 	// kind-specific config key whose values are paths that a device pod cannot
517: 	// see unless they are staged into the node's ConfigMaps first.
518: 	pathFields := map[string]bool{
519: 		"copy-to-flash": true,
520: 	}
521: 
522: 	for _, entry := range config.Topology.GetNodeKindSpecificConfig(nodeName) {
523: 		if !pathFields[entry.Key] {
524: 			continue
525: 		}
526: 
527: 		paths, ok := entry.Value.([]any)
528: 		if !ok {
529: 			continue
530: 		}
531: 
532: 		pathsByField[entry.Key] = make([]string, 0, len(paths))
533: 		for _, p := range paths {
534: 			if s, ok := p.(string); ok {
535: 				pathsByField[entry.Key] = append(pathsByField[entry.Key], s)
536: 			}
537: 		}
538: 	}
539: 
540: 	fieldNames := make([]string, 0, len(pathsByField))
541: 	for fieldName := range pathsByField {
542: 		fieldNames = append(fieldNames, fieldName)
543: 	}
544: 	sort.Strings(fieldNames)
545: 
546: 	for _, fieldName := range fieldNames {
547: 		for _, sourcePath := range pathsByField[fieldName] {
548: 			if strings.TrimSpace(sourcePath) == "" {
549: 				continue
550: 			}
551: 
552: 			if err := stageSourcePathIntoConfigMaps(
553: 				configMaps,
554: 				topologyName,
555: 				sourcePath,
556: 				nodeName,
557: 				topologyFileDir,
558: 				topologyLabDir,
559: 			); err != nil {
560: 				return fmt.Errorf(
561: 					"failed staging %s path %q for node %q: %w",
562: 					fieldName,
563: 					sourcePath,
564: 					nodeName,
565: 					err,
566: 				)
567: 			}
568: 		}
569: 	}
570: 
571: 	return nil
572: }

574: func stageSourcePathIntoConfigMaps(
575: 	configMaps map[string]*stagedConfigMap,

594: 			return err

Reasoning
So staging functions all go through `GetNode*` accessors that guard nil. Thus a topology with a null node body (e.g., `node1:` followed by nothing) would reach `sanitizeNodeNames` with a nil `*NodeDefinition` in `config.Topology.Nodes`, and the new `nodeDefinition.Labels[...]` would panic. The types package and files.go consistently treat nil node definitions as possible (guards at topology.go lines 31, 83, 128 and files.go 337/364/404). This is a genuine P2 correctness defect introduced by this PR.

Wait — but is a null-valued node entry realistic? YAML:

```yaml
name: lab1
topology:
  nodes:
    node1:
  kinds: ...
```

yaml.v3 unmarshals `node1:` with empty value into `map[string]*NodeDefinition` as nil pointer. Yes, that's standard yaml.v3 behavior. Then other parts of containerlab (the main parser) might reject such topologies earlier? The clabernetes runtime parses `req.TopologyDefinition` itself via yaml.Unmarshal into clabRuntimeConfig (files.go line 123). But is the deploy request's TopologyDefinition validated earlier (e.g., by the common config parse) before reaching the runtime? In the clabernetes runtime, Deploy receives the request; whether an earlier parse already validated the topology is unclear. But even if the main parse would fail on a null node... containerlab's main parser builds nodes from the topology; a null node definition might be an error "missing kind" or might work. Let me check how the test suite/parse handles it. Actually, the defensive nil checks in `getField` explicitly document the possibility ("if the node definition is not nil"), meaning this shape is accepted somewhere in the codebase. Whether it reaches this path depends on the deploy flow. Let me check where DeployRequest.TopologyDefinition is produced for the clabernetes runtime — lifecycle.go line 32 checks `len(req.TopologyDefinition) == 0`. Let me look at how the clabernetes runtime gets its definition; maybe from the raw file. If the main config parse happens first and would reject null nodes... Let me search for how node definitions are consumed in the main flow — clab parses topology via `types.Topology` too? The main flow uses the same `types.Topology` with `map[string]*NodeDefinition`. If a null node was fatal earlier, the guards in getField would be pointless.

Hmm, actually a null node body may also arise from a different path: nodes defined only under `groups` or with kind reference? No — the map is just the `nodes:` section.

Let me quickly test my yaml.v3 claim: yes, unmarshalling `nodes:\n  a:\n` into `map[string]*NodeDefinition` yields `{"a": nil}`. That's well-known behavior.

Does containerlab's normal (non-c9s) deploy reject this earlier? In main flow, node definitions are loaded via clabconfig... Let me check if there's a validation that requires kind. Node kind defaults to linux. A null definition is probably valid (all defaults). E.g. `node1:` with nothing — containerlab likely accepts it (defaults apply). Let me check how the CLI parses nodes — `ParseNodeConfig` or similar in config package.

Reasoning
The request carries `c.renderedTopology` — a rendered topology (possibly with env-var substitution). It's parsed by the clabernetes runtime with yaml.Unmarshal. So whether a null node body reaches here depends on whether the main CLab parse would already fail. The main parse path builds node configs from the same parsed topology. Would `node1:` (null) fail in main parse? Node kind defaults to "linux" — the `GetNodeKind` returns "linux" default. So the main flow likely accepts it. So the nil case is realistic.

But wait — I should double check yaml.v3 behavior for `nodes:\n    node1:` (null value) into `map[string]*NodeDefinition`. yaml.v3: unmarshalling a null into a pointer/map value sets it to zero value (nil for pointer). Yes, this is correct — known behavior.

However, would the clabernetes runtime even get there? The main parse of the topology file happens in CLab.Parse (core). Let me verify there is no earlier validation that rejects null node bodies. Actually, whether or not the main flow rejects, the clabernetes runtime path parses the rendered topology itself; the c9s path is used when the lab runtime is c9s — the main flow still parses the topology first to build c.Config (name etc.) and c.renderedTopology. If the main flow accepts null nodes (likely, since `getField` guards for it — those guards were added for some reason), the c9s deploy would panic.

Hmm, but actually, why would getField guard for nil? Maybe defensive coding only. Let me check whether there's any test in the codebase covering null node definitions. Search for a test with a null node, e.g., "\n    node1:\n" patterns in YAML test strings. Hard. Let me instead check whether containerlab's main parse loop would panic elsewhere with a nil NodeDefinition — e.g., core.Parse iterating topo.Nodes and calling node fields. Search core for `range.*\.Nodes`.

112: 			nodeRuntimes[nodeName] = r
113: 			continue
114: 		}
115: 
116: 		// this case if for non-default runtimes overriding the global default
117: 		if topologyNode != nil {
118: 			if r, ok := clabnodes.NonDefaultRuntimes[topologyNode.Kind]; ok {
119: 				nodeRuntimes[nodeName] = r
120: 				continue
121: 			}
122: 		}
123: 
124: 		// saving the global default runtime
125: 		nodeRuntimes[nodeName] = c.globalRuntimeName
126: 	}
127: 
128: 	// initialize any extra runtimes
129: 	for _, r := range nodeRuntimes {
130: 		// this is the case for already init'ed runtimes
131: 		if _, ok := c.Runtimes[r]; ok {
132: 			continue
133: 		}
134: 
135: 		if rInit, ok := clabruntime.ContainerRuntimes[r]; ok {
136: 			newRuntime := rInit()
137: 			defaultConfig := c.Runtimes[c.globalRuntimeName].Config()
138: 
139: 			err := newRuntime.Init(
140: 				clabruntime.WithConfig(&defaultConfig),
141: 			)
142: 			if err != nil {
143: 				return fmt.Errorf("failed to init the container runtime: %s", err)
144: 			}
145: 
146: 			c.Runtimes[r] = newRuntime
147: 		}
148: 	}
149: 
150: 	for idx, nodeName := range nodeNames {
151: 		err = c.NewNode(nodeName, nodeRuntimes[nodeName], c.Config.Topology.Nodes[nodeName], idx)
152: 		if err != nil {
153: 			return err
154: 		}
155: 	}
156: 
157: 	return c.injectTailscaleSidecars()
158: }

Reasoning
Confirmed: the main flow handles nil node definitions fine (uses only `GetNode*` accessors which guard nil). So a topology like:

```yaml
name: lab1
topology:
  nodes:
    node1:
```

is valid in the main flow, and deploying it with the clabernetes runtime would reach `sanitizeNodeNames`, where the new `nodeDefinition.Labels[labelOriginalNodeName]` dereferences a nil pointer → panic. This is a solid P2 (crash on valid input; new code; existing guards elsewhere in the package show the nil case is expected). files.go even has `if nodeDefinition == nil { nodeDefinition = &clabtypes.NodeDefinition{}; ... }` guards. So the fix: skip label stamping when `nodeDefinition == nil` (and keep the nil entry in the map), matching the existing pattern.

Wait, also verify: does `renderClabernetesTopologyDefinition` marshal a nil node? If definitionChanged true (it will be true since a rename/label... in this panic case we never get there). But if we fix by skipping, a nil definition in Nodes rendered as nil → yaml marshals "node1: null". Base behavior already had this (nodes map with nil values rendered when renamed). Fine.

Actually — hold on. Also double-check that yaml.v3 null map value for `map[string]*NodeDefinition` is indeed nil. Yes: for a null node, yaml.v3 sets pointer to nil.

Now, is there also a panic risk in `renderClabernetesTopologyDefinition` or `clabernetesBriefLinks`? Pre-existing. Not our concern.

Second potential finding: `TestSanitizeLabelValue` uses `t.Parallel()` inside `t.Run` subtests — fine.

Third: go.mod bump of clabernetes from 0.10.0 to 0.10.1 — the doc comment says the compiler stamps the same label on Nodes it renames itself. Can't verify external lib. The `labelOriginalNodeName` const in clabernetes.go is defined here and used in tests; fine.

Fourth check: the `definitionChanged` semantics. Previously, `sanitizeNodeNames` returned renames, and files.go checked `len(renames) != 0` to decide re-render. Now it uses `definitionsChanged`. In base, `if len(renames) != 0 { definitionChanged = true }`. Now `if definitionsChanged { definitionChanged = true }`. Equivalent plus label additions. Good.

Fifth: potential double-stamping interplay with the external compiler. The doc says the compiler stamps the same label on Nodes it renames itself. But here containerlab stamps the label in the *node definition* (inside the topology spec), not on the resulting Node object labels... wait, does the definition's `labels` field get applied to the compiled Node object metadata labels by the compiler? The test asserts `getTestPrimitive(t, r, nodeGVR, ...).GetLabels()[labelOriginalNodeName]`, so yes, node definition labels become Node object labels in this runtime's primitive compilation path. And the Topology resource path compiles in-cluster; the label in the definition rides along. OK.

Sixth: label value case preservation — but wait, is `c9s.run/originalNodeName` with value "R1" acceptable to K8s? Yes (63 chars, alnum). Good.

Seventh check — `SanitizeLabelValue` idempotency edge: name = "node one" → replace " " with "-" → "node-one" → valid. Good. name = "srv6+flexalgo" → "srv6-flexalgo". Good. What about a name that is ≤63 and matches but is a single invalid char... covered.

Eighth: an edge in `SanitizeLabelValue` truncation: `sanitized[:kubernetesNameMaxLen-nameDigestLen-1]` = [:55], then TrimRight of "-_." — trimming may shorten below 55, making total < 63. Fine, still valid. But: TrimRight could trim all the way back to "" if the first 55 chars are all in "-_."? Can sanitized (after the earlier trim) begin with "-_."? No — the earlier `strings.Trim(..., "-_.")` removes leading chars in that set, so sanitized[0] is alphanumeric. So TrimRight leaves at least 1 char. Result ≥ 1 char, valid. Good.

But one more: after the earlier Trim, could `sanitized` contain only valid chars but fail the leading/trailing rule? Trim removes trailing too. So the non-truncation path always returns a valid label. And the quick-return path returns name only if it matches the valid regex. Good.

Ninth: `SanitizeLabelValue` doesn't apply the digest on names that fail the "starts/ends alphanumeric" check after replacement... covered by Trim.

Now, potential issue with collision between SanitizeLabelValue output and SanitizeNodeNames? Not needed.

Tenth: `TestFreshTopologyDeployKeepsTopologyChangedConcurrently` — stored definition now includes labels for sros and vsrx; fine.

Eleventh: check `getTestPrimitive` in the test for "r1-console" — its label equals "r1-console" since name is unchanged and valid.

Output

<path>/source/head/docs/manual/clabernetes/runtime.md</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
200: containerlab --runtime c9s --namespace default deploy -t clos.clab.yml
201: ```
202: 
203: All single-lab lifecycle commands must use the same flag or environment
204: override. Because the namespace is then shared, node and other resource names
205: can conflict between labs.
206: 
207: /// note | Lab and node names
208: Kubernetes object names are lowercase DNS labels, and a lot of labs name their
209: nodes `R1` or `PE_1`. Containerlab sanitizes the lab name and every node name
210: Kubernetes cannot carry instead of rejecting the topology: the name is
211: lower-cased, every character outside `a-z`, `0-9` and `-` becomes `-`, a name
212: that would start with a digit is prefixed with `clab-`, and a name longer than
213: 63 characters is truncated and suffixed with a hash. `R1` becomes `r1`, `PE_1`
214: becomes `pe-1`.
215: 
216: Every rename is logged, and the topology is rewritten before it reaches the
217: cluster, so links, `network-mode: container:<node>` and the staged file mounts
218: follow the node. Node names on the command line (`node start R1`,
219: `--node-filter R1`, `save`) are accepted in either form. Everything
220: containerlab reports back — `inspect`, container names, the `clab-node-name`
221: label — uses the sanitized name, because that is the name the objects carry in
222: the cluster.
223: 
224: Two node names that differ only in a character Kubernetes cannot carry (`R1`
225: and `r1`) would end up as one object; that is an error, not a merge.
226: 
227: `c9s-<lab-name>` must still fit in a DNS label of 63 characters, which leaves
228: at most 59 characters for the lab name.
229: ///
230: 
231: Some commands intentionally look across namespaces:
232: 
233: - `inspect --all`
234: - `destroy --all`
235: - `events`
236: 
237: For these commands, containerlab uses all-namespaces kubernetes listing or
238: watching. The synthetic c9s container ID includes the namespace so follow-up
239: actions can still target the right lab:
240: 
241: ```text
242: <namespace>/<lab>/<node>
243: ```
244:

2: description: Verify release-note mutability when reviewing PR documentation
3: alwaysApply: true

5: 
6: # Release-note review
7: 
8: When reviewing a PR, inspect any changes under `docs/rn/`:
9: 
10: - Verify the target release is still unreleased before treating the change as valid.
11: - Treat release notes for published releases as immutable; flag edits or additions there.
12: - Recommend moving new content to the next unreleased release note or the appropriate evergreen documentation.

(End of file - total 12 lines)

2: description: Require explicit user authorization for Git mutations
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Git mutation policy
7: 
8: - Never automatically commit, amend, push, force-push, or create/update a pull request.
9: - Leave ordinary fixes as working-tree changes unless the user explicitly requests the specific Git or GitHub action.
10: - A request to implement or fix code alone does not authorize a commit or push.

Reasoning
Now, one more consideration: the PR stores the definition with the injected `c9s.run/originalNodeName` labels. This changes what the Topology CR `spec.definition.containerlab` contains vs. the user's file. Does anything in this repo (or the c9s runtime) compare the stored definition to the file-rendered one to detect drift/no-op plans? Look at plan.go — `Plan` computes whether the definition changed by comparing existing Topology's definition with the newly computed one. If the comparison is between the raw file and the stored (labeled) definition, then on a re-plan of an unchanged lab, the newly computed definition (with labels) equals the stored one — fine. But what about labs deployed with the *previous* containerlab version (stored definition without labels)? Re-plan with new version → computed (labeled) differs from stored (unlabeled) → considered changed → redeploy. That's a one-time migration, probably acceptable/intended.

More interesting: does the plan/drift logic use the raw `req.TopologyDefinition`? Let me check plan.go around the comparison.

Now the important one: `topologyObject` puts the definition into the Topology CR; reconcile.go's `resourceConforms` compares labels/annotations/spec... The label rides inside spec.definition string. Since both desired and existing go through the same code path, no churn after first deploy. Good.

Next: check `topology.go` line 204: `for nodeName, spec := range parsed.Topology.Nodes` — is there a nil deref there pre-existing? Not this PR's concern.

Now, let me reconsider the nil-pointer severity. Trigger: user deploys a topology where a node has an empty body (`node1:` with nothing under it) with the c9s runtime. Does the main flow's parse succeed? createNodeCfg uses GetNode* accessors which guard nil (getField line 31 `if nodeDefinition != nil`), GetNodePrivileged (getFieldPtr line 83 guards), so yes, main flow accepts. Then `c.renderedTopology` — what's rendered? It's rendered from the parsed config? "renderedTopology" is probably the topology file with env substitution rendered. Let me check how c.renderedTopology is produced — if it re-renders from NodeConfig, a nil-definition node might produce a full definition anyway. Let me grep renderedTopology.
